<p><b>John Russell, 1st Earl Russell</b>, <span class="noexcerpt nowraplinks" style="font-size:100%; font-weight:normal;"><a href="/wiki/Knight_of_the_Order_of_the_Garter" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight of the Order of the Garter">KG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Knight_Grand_Cross_of_the_Order_of_St_Michael_and_St_George" class="mw-redirect" title="Knight Grand Cross of the Order of St Michael and St George">GCMG</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Privy_Council_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Privy Council of the United Kingdom">PC</a><span style="font-size:14px;">&#44;</span>&#32;<a href="/wiki/Fellow_of_the_Royal_Society" title="Fellow of the Royal Society">FRS</a></span> (18 August 1792 – 28 May 1878), known by his <a href="/wiki/Courtesy_titles_in_the_United_Kingdom" title="Courtesy titles in the United Kingdom">courtesy title</a> <b>Lord John Russell</b> before 1861, was a British <a href="/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party)" title="Whigs (British political party)">Whig</a> and <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal</a> statesman who served as <a href="/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Prime Minister of the United Kingdom">Prime Minister of the United Kingdom</a> from 1846 to 1852 and again from 1865 to 1866.
</p><p>The third son of the <a href="/wiki/John_Russell,_6th_Duke_of_Bedford" title="John Russell, 6th Duke of Bedford">6th Duke of Bedford</a>, Russell was educated at <a href="/wiki/Westminster_School" title="Westminster School">Westminster School</a> and Edinburgh University before entering Parliament in 1813. In 1828 he took a leading role in the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Test_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Test Act">Test Acts</a> which discriminated against Catholics and Protestant dissenters. He was one of the principal architects of the <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1832" title="Reform Act 1832">Reform Act 1832</a>, which was the first major reform of Parliament since the <a href="/wiki/Stuart_Restoration" title="Stuart Restoration">Restoration</a>, and a significant early step on the road to democracy and away from rule by the aristocracy and <a href="/wiki/Landed_gentry" title="Landed gentry">landed gentry</a>. He favoured expanding the right to vote to the <a href="/wiki/Middle_class" title="Middle class">middle classes</a> and enfranchising Britain's growing industrial towns and cities, but he never advocated universal suffrage and he opposed the secret ballot. Russell was outspoken on many issues over the course of his career, advocating <a href="/wiki/Catholic_emancipation" title="Catholic emancipation">Catholic emancipation</a> in the 1820s, calling for the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a> in 1845, denouncing <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_IX" title="Pope Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a>'s <a href="/wiki/Universalis_Ecclesiae" title="Universalis Ecclesiae">revival of Catholic bishoprics</a> in 1850, and supporting <a href="/wiki/Italian_unification" class="mw-redirect" title="Italian unification">Italian unification</a> during the 1860s.
</p><p>Russell's ministerial career spanned four decades. In addition to his two terms as prime minister, between 1831 and 1865 he served in the cabinets of <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Earl Grey</a>, <a href="/wiki/William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne">Viscount Melbourne</a>, the <a href="/wiki/George_Hamilton-Gordon,_4th_Earl_of_Aberdeen" title="George Hamilton-Gordon, 4th Earl of Aberdeen">Earl of Aberdeen</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Henry_John_Temple,_3rd_Viscount_Palmerston" title="Henry John Temple, 3rd Viscount Palmerston">Viscount Palmerston</a>. Russell's relationship with Palmerston was often stormy and contributed to bringing down Russell's first government in 1852 and Palmerston's first government in 1858. However, their renewed alliance from 1859 was one of the foundations of the united Liberal Party, which would go on to dominate British politics in the following decades. While Russell was an energetic and effective minister during the 1830s and helped to commit the Whigs to a reform agenda, he proved less successful as prime minister. During his two periods as prime minister he often suffered from a disunited cabinet and weak support in the House of Commons, meaning he was unable to carry out much of his agenda. During his first premiership, his government failed to deal effectively with the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Famine" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Famine">Irish Famine</a>, a disaster that saw the loss of a quarter of Ireland's population through death and emigration. During his second premiership, he split his party by pressing for further parliamentary reform and was forced from office only to watch <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Derby</a> and <a href="/wiki/Disraeli" class="mw-redirect" title="Disraeli">Disraeli</a> carry a more ambitious <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1867" title="Reform Act 1867">Reform Bill</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECannonCrowcroft2015827_1-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECannonCrowcroft2015827-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> It has been said that Russell's <a href="/wiki/First_Russell_ministry" title="First Russell ministry">ministry of 1846–1852</a> was the ruin of the old Whig party and that his <a href="/wiki/Liberal_government,_1859%E2%80%931866" title="Liberal government, 1859–1866">ministry of 1865–1866</a> was very nearly the ruin of the Liberal Party that took its place.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor197667_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor197667-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Background_and_early_life">Background and early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Background and early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Russell was born on 18 August 1792 into the highest echelons of the British aristocracy, being the third son of <a href="/wiki/John_Russell,_6th_Duke_of_Bedford" title="John Russell, 6th Duke of Bedford">John Russell, later 6th Duke of Bedford</a>, and Georgiana Byng, daughter of <a href="/wiki/George_Byng,_4th_Viscount_Torrington" title="George Byng, 4th Viscount Torrington">George Byng, 4th Viscount Torrington</a>. <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Bedford" title="Duke of Bedford">The Russell family</a> had been one of the principal Whig dynasties in England since the 17th century, and were among the richest handful of aristocratic landowning families in the country, but as a younger son of the <a href="/wiki/John_Russell,_6th_Duke_of_Bedford" title="John Russell, 6th Duke of Bedford">6th Duke of Bedford</a>, he was not expected to inherit the family estates. As a younger son of a duke, he bore the courtesy title "Lord John Russell", but he was not a peer in his own right. He was, therefore, able to sit in the House of Commons until he was made an earl in 1861 and was elevated to the House of Lords.
</p><p>Russell was born two months premature and was small and sickly as a child (even in adulthood he remained under 5&#160;feet 5&#160;inches (1.65&#160;m) in height, and his small stature was frequently the butt of jokes by political opponents and caricaturists).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> In 1801 at the age of nine he was sent away to school. Shortly thereafter his mother died. After being withdrawn from <a href="/wiki/Westminster_School" title="Westminster School">Westminster School</a> in 1804 due to ill health, Russell was educated by tutors, including <a href="/wiki/Edmund_Cartwright" title="Edmund Cartwright">Edmund Cartwright</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer19995_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer19995-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> In 1806 Russell's father was made <a href="/wiki/Lord_Lieutenant_of_Ireland" title="Lord Lieutenant of Ireland">Lord Lieutenant of Ireland</a> in the short-live <a href="/wiki/Ministry_of_All_the_Talents" title="Ministry of All the Talents">Ministry of All the Talents</a> and it was during this time that the young Russell met <a href="/wiki/Charles_James_Fox" title="Charles James Fox">Charles James Fox</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid18959_5-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid18959-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Fox was Russell's formative political hero and would remain an inspiration throughout his life.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Russell attended the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Edinburgh" title="University of Edinburgh">University of Edinburgh</a> from 1809 to 1812, lodging with Professor <a href="/wiki/John_Playfair" title="John Playfair">John Playfair</a>, who oversaw his studies.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid189512_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid189512-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> He did not take a degree. Although often in poor health, he travelled widely in Britain and in <a href="/wiki/Continental_Europe" title="Continental Europe">Continental Europe</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest197211-13_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest197211-13-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> and held commission as Captain in the Bedfordshire Militia in 1810.<sup id="cite_ref-hop_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-hop-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> During his continental travels, Russell visited Spain where his <a href="/wiki/Lord_George_Russell" title="Lord George Russell">brother</a> was serving as aide-de-camp to <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Wellesley,_1st_Duke_of_Wellington" title="Arthur Wellesley, 1st Duke of Wellington">Lord Wellington</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Peninsular_War" title="Peninsular War">Peninsular War</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a62–76_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a62–76-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> The following year, Russell had a 90-minute meeting with <a href="/wiki/Napoleon" title="Napoleon">Napoleon</a> in December 1814, during the former emperor's exile at <a href="/wiki/Elba" title="Elba">Elba</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a74–75_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a74–75-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_political_career">Early political career</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Early political career">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="Backbench_MP:_1813.E2.80.931830"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Backbench_MP:_1813–1830">Backbench MP: 1813–1830</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Backbench MP: 1813–1830">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Russell entered the <a href="/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="House of Commons of the United Kingdom">House of Commons</a> as a <a href="/wiki/Whigs_(British_political_party)" title="Whigs (British political party)">Whig</a> in 1813 at the age of 20. The future reformer gained his seat by virtue of his father, the <a href="/wiki/6th_Duke_of_Bedford" class="mw-redirect" title="6th Duke of Bedford">Duke of Bedford</a>, instructing the 30 or so electors of Tavistock to return him as an MP even though at the time Russell was abroad and under age.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a69–70_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a69–70-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Russell entered Parliament more out of a sense of duty and family tradition than out of serious political ambition. With the exception the 1806-1807 coalition government in which Russell's father had served, the Whigs had been out of power since 1783, and Russell could have had had no certain expectation of a ministerial career. In June 1815, Russell denounced the <a href="/wiki/Bourbon_Restoration_in_France" title="Bourbon Restoration in France">Bourbon Restoration</a> and Britain's declaration of war against the <a href="/wiki/Hundred_Days" title="Hundred Days">recently-returned Napoleon</a> by arguing in the House of Commons that foreign powers had no right to dictate France's form of government.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1817, tired of the prospect of perpetual opposition, Russell resigned from Parliament. After spending a year out of politics and travelling on the continent, he changed his mind and re-entered Parliament for Tavistock at the <a href="/wiki/1818_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1818 United Kingdom general election">1818 general election</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer199919_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer199919-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> In 1819, Russell embraced the cause of parliamentary reform and he led the more reformist wing of the Whigs throughout the 1820s. In 1828, while still an opposition <a href="/wiki/Backbencher" title="Backbencher">backbencher</a>, Russell introduced a <a href="/wiki/Sacramental_Test_Act_1828" title="Sacramental Test Act 1828">Sacramental Test bill</a> with the aim of abolishing the prohibitions on Catholics and Protestant dissenters being elected to local government and from holding civil and military offices. The bill gained the backing of the Tory <a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">Home Secretary</a> <a href="/wiki/Sir_Robert_Peel" class="mw-redirect" title="Sir Robert Peel">Sir Robert Peel</a> and was passed into law.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Minister_under_Grey_and_Melbourne:_1830.E2.80.931841"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Minister_under_Grey_and_Melbourne:_1830–1841">Minister under Grey and Melbourne: 1830–1841</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Minister under Grey and Melbourne: 1830–1841">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>When the Whigs came to power in 1830, Russell entered <a href="/wiki/Charles_Grey,_2nd_Earl_Grey" title="Charles Grey, 2nd Earl Grey">Earl Grey's</a> government as <a href="/wiki/Paymaster_of_the_Forces" title="Paymaster of the Forces">Paymaster of the Forces</a>. Despite being a relatively junior minister, as a vocal advocate for Parliamentary reform for over a decade, Russell became a principal leader in the fight for the <a href="/wiki/Reform_Act_1832" title="Reform Act 1832">Reform Act 1832</a>. He was one of the committee of four tasked by Grey with drafting the reform bill, alongside cabinet ministers <a href="/wiki/John_Lambton,_1st_Earl_of_Durham" title="John Lambton, 1st Earl of Durham">Lord Durham</a>, <a href="/wiki/John_Ponsonby,_4th_Earl_of_Bessborough" title="John Ponsonby, 4th Earl of Bessborough">Lord Duncannon</a> and <a href="/wiki/Sir_James_Graham,_2nd_Baronet" title="Sir James Graham, 2nd Baronet">Sir James Graham</a>. Despite not yet being in the Cabinet, Russell was chosen to introduce the bill in March 1831 and over the following year he successfully steered the Reform Act's difficult progress through the Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer199944–52_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer199944–52-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Russell earned the nickname "Finality Jack" from his pronouncing the Act a final measure but in later years he would go on to push for further reform of Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> In May 1834, Russell made a speech on the Irish Tithes bill, in which he argued that the revenue generated by tithes was more than was justified by the size of the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_Ireland" title="Church of Ireland">established Protestant church in Ireland</a>. Russell argued that a proportion the tithe revenue should instead be appropriated for the education of the Irish poor, regardless of denomination.<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The speech was seen by its opponents as an attack on the established church in Ireland and it cemented a split within Grey's government over the issue of Irish tithes.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a208–209_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a208–209-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> The following month <a href="/wiki/Derby_Dilly" title="Derby Dilly">four members of the Cabinet</a> resigned over the issue, weakening the government's hold on Parliament.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> Sensing that his position was now hopeless, Grey offered his resignation to the King in July, and was replaced by <a href="/wiki/William_Lamb,_2nd_Viscount_Melbourne" title="William Lamb, 2nd Viscount Melbourne">Viscount Melbourne</a> at the head of the government.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Russel_Purge;_etching_by_CJG,_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/The_Russel_Purge%3B_etching_by_CJG%2C_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg/250px-The_Russel_Purge%3B_etching_by_CJG%2C_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg" decoding="async" width="250" height="179" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/The_Russel_Purge%3B_etching_by_CJG%2C_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg/375px-The_Russel_Purge%3B_etching_by_CJG%2C_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/92/The_Russel_Purge%3B_etching_by_CJG%2C_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg/500px-The_Russel_Purge%3B_etching_by_CJG%2C_1831._Wellcome_L0006863.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1654" data-file-height="1182" /></a><figcaption>A pro-reform cartoon of 1831 depicting Russell as the man with the cure for the country's ills. Tory MPs for <a href="/wiki/Rotten_and_pocket_boroughs" title="Rotten and pocket boroughs">rotten and pocket boroughs</a> are shown as patients requiring "The Russell Purge".</figcaption></figure>
<p>In November 1834, when the leader of the Commons, <a href="/wiki/John_Spencer,_3rd_Earl_Spencer" title="John Spencer, 3rd Earl Spencer">Lord Althorp</a>, succeeded to the peerage as <a href="/wiki/Earl_Spencer_(peerage)" class="mw-redirect" title="Earl Spencer (peerage)">Earl Spencer</a>, Russell became the leader of the Whigs in the Commons. Russell's appointment prompted <a href="/wiki/King_William_IV" class="mw-redirect" title="King William IV">King William IV</a> to terminate Melbourne's government, in part because the King objected to Russell's views on the Irish Church.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a217–218_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a217–218-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup> This remains the last time in British history that a monarch has dismissed a government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHawkins2007152_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHawkins2007152-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> The subsequent minority Conservative government lasted less than five months before resigning in April 1835. Russell then returned to office as <a href="/wiki/Home_Secretary" title="Home Secretary">Home Secretary</a> in <a href="/wiki/Second_Melbourne_ministry" title="Second Melbourne ministry">Melbourne's second government</a>, before serving as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_War_and_the_Colonies" title="Secretary of State for War and the Colonies">Secretary of State for War and the Colonies</a> from 1839 to 1841. Through this period, he continued to lead the more reformist wing of the Whig party.
</p><p>As Home Secretary, Russell recommended and secured royal pardons for the <a href="/wiki/Tolpuddle_Martyrs" title="Tolpuddle Martyrs">Tolpuddle Martyrs</a> and partial commutation of their sentences.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup> In 1836, he introduced the <a href="/wiki/Marriage_Act_1836" title="Marriage Act 1836">Marriages Act</a>, which introduced civil marriages in England and Wales and allowed Catholics and Protestant Dissenters to marry in their own churches.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1837, he steered a series of seven Acts through Parliament, which together reduced the number of offences carrying a sentence of death from thirty-seven to sixteen.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup> This number was reduced further by the <a href="/wiki/Substitution_of_Punishments_of_Death_Act_1841" title="Substitution of Punishments of Death Act 1841">Substitution of Punishments of Death Act 1841</a>. After these reforms the death penalty was rarely used in the United Kingdom for crimes other than murder. As Home Secretary Russell also introduced the public registration for births, marriages and deaths and played a large role in democratising the government of cities outside of London.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Opposition:_1841.E2.80.931846"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Opposition:_1841–1846">Opposition: 1841–1846</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Opposition: 1841–1846">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1841 the Whigs lost the <a href="/wiki/1841_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1841 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> to the Conservatives and Russell and his colleagues returned to opposition. In November 1845, following the failure of that year's potato harvest across Britain and Ireland, Russell came out in favour of the repeal of the <a href="/wiki/Corn_Laws" title="Corn Laws">Corn Laws</a> and called upon the Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Robert_Peel" title="Robert Peel">Sir Robert Peel</a> to take urgent action to alleviate the emerging food crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Peel had by this time already become convinced of the need for repeal, but he was opposed in this by the majority of his own cabinet and party. On 11 December 1845, frustrated by his party's unwillingness to support him on repeal, Peel resigned as Prime Minister and <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a> invited Russell to form a new government. With the Whigs a minority in the Commons however, Russell struggled to assemble the necessary support. When <a href="/wiki/Henry_George_Grey,_3rd_Earl_Grey" class="mw-redirect" title="Henry George Grey, 3rd Earl Grey">Lord Grey</a> declared that he would not serve in cabinet if <a href="/wiki/Lord_Palmerston" class="mw-redirect" title="Lord Palmerston">Lord Palmerston</a> was made Foreign Secretary, it became clear to Russell that he could not form a viable government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a410–416_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a410–416-28">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Russell declined the Queen's invitation on 21 December and Peel agreed to stay on as Prime Minister. In June 1846, Peel repealed the Corn Laws with Whig support, bitterly dividing the Conservative Party in the process. Later that same night Peel's <a href="/wiki/Coercion_Act" title="Coercion Act">Irish Coercion Bill</a> was defeated after vengeful anti-repeal Tories voted with the opposition; and Peel, taking this as a vote of no confidence, resigned as Prime Minister. Russell accepted the Queen's offer to form a government; this time Grey did not object to Palmerston's appointment.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a422–424_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a422–424-29">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister:_1846.E2.80.931852"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister:_1846–1852">Prime Minister: 1846–1852<span class="anchor" id="First_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Prime Minister: 1846–1852">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Appointment">Appointment</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Appointment">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/First_Russell_ministry" title="First Russell ministry">First Russell ministry</a></div>
<p>Russell took office as Prime Minister with the Whigs only a minority in the House of Commons. It was the bitter split in the Conservative Party over the Corn Laws that allowed Russell's government to remain in power in spite of this, with Sir Robert Peel and his supporters offering tentative support to the new ministry in order to keep the protectionist Conservatives under <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Lord Stanley</a> in opposition. At the <a href="/wiki/1847_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1847 United Kingdom general election">general election of August 1847</a> the Whigs made gains at the expense of the Conservatives, but remained a minority, with Russell's government still dependent on the votes of <a href="/wiki/Peelite" title="Peelite">Peelite</a> and <a href="/wiki/Repeal_Association" title="Repeal Association">Irish Repealer</a> MPs to win divisions in the Commons.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Domestic_agenda">Domestic agenda</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Domestic agenda">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="Group portrait of the Royal Commissioners of the Great Exhibition by Henry Wyndham Phillips" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg/350px-The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg" decoding="async" width="350" height="208" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg/525px-The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b7/The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg/700px-The_Royal_Commissioners_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2500" data-file-height="1487" /></a><figcaption>Russell served on the <a href="/wiki/Royal_Commission_for_the_Exhibition_of_1851" title="Royal Commission for the Exhibition of 1851">Royal Commission</a> for the <a href="/wiki/Great_Exhibition" title="Great Exhibition">Great Exhibition</a>, which took place in 1851 while he was Prime Minister. In this group portrait of the Commissioners, by <a href="/wiki/Henry_Wyndham_Phillips" title="Henry Wyndham Phillips">Henry Wyndham Phillips</a>, Russell is depicted standing behind <a href="/wiki/Albert,_Prince_Consort" class="mw-redirect" title="Albert, Prince Consort">Prince Albert</a> (fifth from right).</figcaption></figure>
<p>Russell's political agenda was frequently frustrated by his lack of a reliable Commons majority. However, his government was able to secure a number of notable social reforms. Russell introduced teachers' pensions and used <a href="/wiki/Order_in_Council" title="Order in Council">Orders in Council</a> to make grants for teacher training. The <a href="/wiki/Baths_and_wash_houses_in_Britain#Regulation" title="Baths and wash houses in Britain">Public Baths and Wash-houses Acts</a> of 1847 and 1848 enabled local authorities to build municipal baths and washing facilities for the growing urban working classes. Russell lent his support to the passage of the <a href="/wiki/Factories_Act_1847" title="Factories Act 1847">Factories Act 1847</a>, which restricted the working hours of women and young persons (aged 13–18) in textile mills to 10 hours per day.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>1848 saw the introduction of the <a href="/wiki/Metropolitan_Commission_of_Sewers" title="Metropolitan Commission of Sewers">Metropolitan Commission of Sewers</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Public_Health_Act_1848" class="mw-redirect" title="Public Health Act 1848">Public Health Act 1848</a>, by which the state assumed responsibility for sewerage, clean water supply, refuse collection and other aspects of public health across much of England and Wales.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a454–455_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889a454–455-30">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Following the election of <a href="/wiki/Lionel_de_Rothschild" title="Lionel de Rothschild">Lionel de Rothschild</a> in the 1847 general election, Russell introduced a Jewish Relief bill, which would have allowed Rothschild and other Jews to sit in the House of Commons without their having to take the explicitly Christian oath of allegiance. In 1848, the bill was passed by the House of Commons, receiving support from the Whigs and a minority of Conservatives (including future Prime Minister <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Benjamin Disraeli</a>). However, it was twice rejected by the Tory dominated House of Lords, as was a new bill in 1851. Rothschild was re-elected in the 1852 general election following the fall of the Russell government but was unable to take his seat until the <a href="/wiki/Jews_Relief_Act_1858" title="Jews Relief Act 1858">Jews Relief Act</a> was finally passed in 1858.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (November 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Ireland">Ireland</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Ireland">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Russell's government led the calamitous response to the <a href="/wiki/Great_Famine_(Ireland)" title="Great Famine (Ireland)">Irish Famine</a>. During the course of the famine, an estimated one million people died from a combination of malnutrition, disease and starvation and well over one million more <a href="/wiki/Irish_diaspora" title="Irish diaspora">emigrated from Ireland</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERoss2002226_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERoss2002226-32">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> After taking office in 1846, Russell's ministry introduced a programme of public works that by the end of that year employed some half-a-million but proved impossible to administer.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELyons197330–34_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELyons197330–34-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In January 1847, the government abandoned this policy, realising that it had failed, and turned to a mixture of "indoor" and "outdoor" direct relief; the former administered in <a href="/wiki/Workhouse" title="Workhouse">workhouses</a> through the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Poor_Laws" title="Irish Poor Laws">Irish Poor Laws</a>, the latter through <a href="/wiki/Soup_kitchen" title="Soup kitchen">soup kitchens</a>. The costs of the Poor Law fell primarily on the local landlords, some of whom in turn attempted to reduce their liability by evicting their tenants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELyons197330–34_33-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELyons197330–34-33">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> In June 1847, the <a href="/wiki/Irish_Poor_Law_Extension_Act" class="mw-redirect" title="Irish Poor Law Extension Act">Poor Law Extension Act</a> was passed, which embodied the principle, popular in Britain, that Irish property should support Irish poverty. Irish landlords were believed in Britain to have created the conditions that led to the famine, a view which Russell shared.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor197677–78_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor197677–78-34">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Financial_crisis">Financial crisis</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Financial crisis">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1847, Russell's government was confronted by a financial crisis.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Sir Robert Peel's 1844 <a href="/wiki/Bank_Charter_Act_1844" title="Bank Charter Act 1844">Bank Charter Act</a> required that all bank notes issued by the <a href="/wiki/Bank_of_England" title="Bank of England">Bank of England</a> be fully <a href="/wiki/Gold_standard" title="Gold standard">backed by gold</a>. However, the failure of harvests in Britain and Ireland during 1846 had led to large outflows of gold in order to pay for imported grain, resulting in a dramatic decline in the Bank's gold reserves over the course of 1847. Faced with the prospect of running out of gold and being unable to issue money, the Bank of England repeatedly raised the discount rate at which it would lend money to other banks, leading to a drastic curtailment of available commercial credit and contributing to the collapse of numerous businesses. This in turn led to a loss of public confidence in the creditworthiness of the banks, culminated in the "week of terror" of 17–23 October when multiple banks were forced to close their doors as frightened depositors <a href="/wiki/Bank_run" title="Bank run">attempted to withdraw their funds</a>. Faced with the potential collapse of the banking system, on Monday 25 October Russell and the Chancellor of the Exchequer <a href="/wiki/Charles_Wood,_1st_Viscount_Halifax" title="Charles Wood, 1st Viscount Halifax">Charles Wood</a> wrote a letter to the Governor of the Bank of England authorising him to break the terms of the Bank Charter Act and issue new notes without gold backing to facilitate lending to other banks. This move restored depositor confidence in the banks, and the crisis abated.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMorganNarron2015_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMorganNarron2015-35">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Relations_with_the_Roman_Catholic_Church">Relations with the Roman Catholic Church</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Relations with the Roman Catholic Church">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the first half of his premiership Russell aimed to improve the British government's relations with the papacy and the Catholic clergy in Ireland, which he saw as one of the keys to making Ireland a more willing part of the United Kingdom. Russell proposed to make an annual grant of £340,000 to the Catholic Church in Ireland, with the aim of ameliorating Irish Catholic opinion towards <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_Union_1800" title="Acts of Union 1800">the Union</a>. In 1847, Russell's father-in-law the <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Elliot-Murray-Kynynmound,_2nd_Earl_of_Minto" title="Gilbert Elliot-Murray-Kynynmound, 2nd Earl of Minto">Earl of Minto</a> was dispatched on a confidential mission to Rome to seek the <a href="/wiki/Pius_IX" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius IX">Pope's</a> support for the grants plan. In the end, the idea had to be abandoned due to Catholic objections to what they saw as an attempt to control their clergy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999158–159_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999158–159-36">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>However, Russell pressed ahead with plans to re-establish formal diplomatic relations between the <a href="/wiki/Court_of_St_James%27s" title="Court of St James&#39;s">Court of St James's</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Holy_See" title="Holy See">Holy See</a>, which had been severed when <a href="/wiki/James_II_of_England" title="James II of England">James II</a> was deposed in 1688. Russell managed to pass an Act to authorise an exchange of ambassadors with Rome, but not before the bill was amended by Parliament to stipulate that the Pope's ambassador must be a <a href="/wiki/Layman" class="mw-redirect" title="Layman">layman</a>. The Pope refused to accept such a restriction on his choice of representative and so the exchange of ambassadors did not take place.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-3" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> It would not be until 1914 that formal <a href="/wiki/Holy_See%E2%80%93United_Kingdom_relations" title="Holy See–United Kingdom relations">UK-Vatican diplomatic relations</a> were finally established.
</p><p>Relations with the papacy soured badly in late 1850 after <a href="/wiki/Pius_IX" class="mw-redirect" title="Pius IX">Pope Pius IX</a> issued the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">bull</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Universalis_Ecclesiae" title="Universalis Ecclesiae">Universalis Ecclesiae</a></i>. By this bull Pius unilaterally reintroduced Catholic bishops to England and Wales for the first time since the <a href="/wiki/English_Reformation" title="English Reformation">Reformation</a>. Anti-Catholic feelings ran high with many Protestants incensed at what they saw as impertinent foreign interference in the prerogative of the established <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> to appoint bishops. Russell, not withstanding his long record of advocating civil liberties for Catholics, shared the traditional Whig suspicion of the Catholic hierarchy, and was angered at what he saw as a papal imposition. On 4 November 1850, in a letter to the <a href="/wiki/Edward_Maltby" title="Edward Maltby">Bishop of Durham</a> published in <i>The Times</i> the same day, Russell wrote that the Pope's actions suggested a "pretension to supremacy" and declared that "No foreign prince or potentate will be permitted to fasten his fetters upon a nation which has so long and so nobly vindicated its right to freedom of opinion, civil, political, and religious". Russell's "Durham letter" won him popular support in England but in Ireland it was viewed as an unwarranted insult to the Pope. It lost Russell the confidence of Irish Repealer MPs and the cabinet were angered that he had made such an incendiary statement without having consulting them.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid1895188–189_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid1895188–189-37">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The following year Russell passed the <a href="/wiki/Ecclesiastical_Titles_Act_1851" title="Ecclesiastical Titles Act 1851">Ecclesiastical Titles Act 1851</a> with Tory support, which made it a criminal offence carrying a fine of £100 for anyone outside of the Church of England to assume an episcopal title "of any city, town or place, or of any territory or district...in the United Kingdom." The Act was widely ignored without consequences and only served to further alienate Irish MPs, thereby weakening the government's position in the Commons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Disagreements_with_Palmerston_and_fall_of_ministry">Disagreements with Palmerston and fall of ministry</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Disagreements with Palmerston and fall of ministry">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Russell frequently clashed with his headstrong Foreign Secretary, <a href="/wiki/Henry_John_Temple,_3rd_Viscount_Palmerston" title="Henry John Temple, 3rd Viscount Palmerston">Lord Palmerston</a>, whose belligerence and support for continental revolution he found embarrassing. In 1847 Palmerston provoked a confrontation with the French government by undermining the plans of the Spanish court to marry the young <a href="/wiki/Isabel_II_of_Spain" class="mw-redirect" title="Isabel II of Spain">Spanish Queen</a> and <a href="/wiki/Infanta_Luisa_Fernanda,_Duchess_of_Montpensier" title="Infanta Luisa Fernanda, Duchess of Montpensier">her sister</a> into the French royal family.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b1–10_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b1–10-38">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
He subsequently clashed with Russell over plans to increase the size of the army and the navy to defend against the perceived threat of French invasion, which subsided after the overthrow of the <a href="/wiki/Louis_Philippe_I" title="Louis Philippe I">French king</a> in 1848.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b13–25_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b13–25-39">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1850 further tension arose between the two over Palmerston's <a href="/wiki/Gunboat_diplomacy" title="Gunboat diplomacy">gunboat diplomacy</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Don_Pacifico_affair_and_case" class="mw-redirect" title="Don Pacifico affair and case">Don Pacifico affair</a> in which Palmerston sought compensation from the Greek government for the ransacking and the burning of the house of <a href="/wiki/Don_Pacifico" class="mw-redirect" title="Don Pacifico">David Pacifico</a>, a Gibraltarian holder of a British passport.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChambers2004313_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChambers2004313-40">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup> Russell considered the matter "hardly worth the interposition of the British lion," and when Palmerston ignored some of his instructions, the Prime Minister wrote to Palmerston telling him he had informed the Queen that he "thought the interests of the country required that a change should take place at the Foreign Department."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b56–60_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b56–60-41">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup> However, less than a month later <a href="/wiki/Edward_Geoffrey_Smith_Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" class="mw-redirect" title="Edward Geoffrey Smith Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Lord Stanley</a> successfully led the House of Lords into passing a motion of censure of the Government over its handling of the affair and Russell realised that he needed to align with Palmerston in order to prevent a similar motion being passed by the House of Commons, which would have obliged the Government to resign.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b61–62_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b61–62-42">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> The Government prevailed, but Palmerston came out of the affair with his popularity at new heights since he was seen as the champion of defending British subjects anywhere in the world.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChambers2004323–4_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChambers2004323–4-43">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Russell forced Palmerston to resign as Foreign Secretary after Palmerston recognised <a href="/wiki/Napoleon_III" title="Napoleon III">Napoleon III</a>'s coup of 2 December 1851 without first consulting the Queen or Cabinet.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup> Russell tried to strengthen his government by recruiting leading Peelites such as <a href="/wiki/Sir_James_Graham,_2nd_Baronet" title="Sir James Graham, 2nd Baronet">Sir James Graham</a> and <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham-Clinton,_5th_Duke_of_Newcastle" title="Henry Pelham-Clinton, 5th Duke of Newcastle">the Duke of Newcastle</a> to his administration, but they declined.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b143_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWalpole1889b143-45">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup> Out of office, Palmerston sought revenge by turning a vote on a militia bill into a <a href="/wiki/Motions_of_no_confidence_in_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Motions of no confidence in the United Kingdom">vote of confidence</a> in the Government. A majority vote in favour of an amendment proposed by Palmerston caused the downfall of Russell's ministry on 21 February 1852. This was Palmerston's famous "tit for tat with Johnny Russell."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Between_premierships">Between premierships</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Between premierships">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span id="In_opposition:_February.E2.80.93December_1852"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="In_opposition:_February–December_1852">In opposition: February–December 1852</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: In opposition: February–December 1852">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Following Russell's resignation, on the 23 February 1852 the <a href="/wiki/Edward_Smith-Stanley,_14th_Earl_of_Derby" title="Edward Smith-Stanley, 14th Earl of Derby">Earl of Derby</a> accepted the Queen's invitation to form a government. The new Conservative ministry were a minority in the Commons due to the continuing rift with the <a href="/wiki/Peelites" class="mw-redirect" title="Peelites">Peelites</a>. Derby called a <a href="/wiki/1852_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1852 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> for July but failed to secure a majority. After the election Derby's Conservatives held 292 out of the 662 seats in the Commons but were able to carry on in office due to divisions among the opposition. Negotiations over a Whig-Peelite coalition stalled over the question of who would lead it. Russell's authority and popularity within the Whigs had been dented by his falling out with Palmerston, who flatly refused to serve under him again. Moreover he had alienated many in the Peelites and the Irish Brigade, who held the balance of power in the Commons, leaving them unwilling to support another Russell-led government. Palmerston proposed <a href="/wiki/Henry_Petty-Fitzmaurice,_3rd_Marquess_of_Lansdowne" title="Henry Petty-Fitzmaurice, 3rd Marquess of Lansdowne">Lord Lansdowne</a> as a compromise candidate. This was acceptable to Russell but Lansdowne was reluctant to take on the burdens of leading a government. The defeat of <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Disraeli's</a> <a href="/wiki/Budget_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Budget of the United Kingdom">Budget</a> in December 1852 forced the issue. Derby's government resigned and the Queen sent for Lansdowne and the Peelite <a href="/wiki/George_Hamilton_Gordon,_4th_Earl_of_Aberdeen" class="mw-redirect" title="George Hamilton Gordon, 4th Earl of Aberdeen">Lord Aberdeen</a>. Lansdowne declined the Queen's invitation, pleading ill-health and so Aberdeen was tasked with forming a government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999195–199_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999195–199-46">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="The_Aberdeen_coalition:_1852.E2.80.931855"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="The_Aberdeen_coalition:_1852–1855">The Aberdeen coalition: 1852–1855</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: The Aberdeen coalition: 1852–1855">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Aberdeen_ministry" title="Aberdeen ministry">Aberdeen ministry</a></div>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img alt="John Russell, 1st Earl Russell, by Sir Francis Grant" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/John_Russell%2C_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg/170px-John_Russell%2C_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="237" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/John_Russell%2C_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg/255px-John_Russell%2C_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7b/John_Russell%2C_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg/340px-John_Russell%2C_1st_Earl_Russell_by_Sir_Francis_Grant_detail.jpg 2x" data-file-width="344" data-file-height="480" /></a><figcaption>Detail of Russell's portrait by <a href="/wiki/Francis_Grant_(artist)" title="Francis Grant (artist)">Francis Grant</a>, 1853</figcaption></figure>
<p>Russell, as the leader of the Whigs, agreed to bring his party into a coalition with the Peelites, headed by Aberdeen. As the leader of the largest party in the coalition, Russell was reluctant to serve under Aberdeen in a subordinate position, but agreed to take on the role of Foreign Secretary on a temporary basis, to lend stability to the fledgling government. He resigned the role in February 1853 in favour of <a href="/wiki/George_Villiers,_4th_Earl_of_Clarendon" title="George Villiers, 4th Earl of Clarendon">Clarendon</a>, but continued to lead for the government in the Commons and attended cabinet without ministerial responsibilities. Russell was unhappy that half of Aberdeen's cabinet was made up of Peelites, despite the fact that the Whigs contributed hundreds of MPs to the Government's support in the Commons, and the Peelites only around 40. However, he came to admire some of his Peelite colleagues, particularly the Chancellor of the Exchequer <a href="/wiki/William_Ewart_Gladstone" title="William Ewart Gladstone">William Gladstone</a>, who would go on to become an important political ally in later years.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999199–204_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999199–204-47">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>With Aberdeen's agreement, Russell used his position as <a href="/wiki/Leader_of_the_House_of_Commons" title="Leader of the House of Commons">Leader of the House of Commons</a> to push for a new Reform Act. Although Russell had promoted the 1832 Reform Act as a one-off measure to re-balance the constitution, after twenty years he had become convinced of the need for further electoral reform. In February 1854 Russell introduced his bill to the House. The property qualification was to be reduced from £10 to £6 in boroughs, and from £50 to £10 in the counties. Additionally 66 seats would be removed from undersized constituencies and redistributed.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999204–206_48-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999204–206-48">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup> The second reading of the bill was set for March 1854, but the prospect of imminent war with Russia led to it being postponed until April. After the outbreak of war on 28 March Russell came under pressure from the cabinet to withdraw the bill entirely. Russell threatened to resign if the cabinet abandoned the reform bill, but he was convinced to stay on by Aberdeen, who promised that he would support the reform bill if Russell reintroduced it in a future session.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid1895240–243_49-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid1895240–243-49">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999208_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999208-50">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup> However, with the fall of the Aberdeen government the following year, it would be 12 years before Russell had another chance to introduce a reform bill.
</p><p>Together with Palmerston, Russell supported the government taking a hard line against Russian territorial ambitions in the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Empire" title="Ottoman Empire">Ottoman Empire</a>, a policy that ultimately resulted in Britain's entry into the <a href="/wiki/Crimean_War" title="Crimean War">Crimean War</a> in March 1854, an outcome that the more cautious Aberdeen had hoped to avoid. In the following months Russell grew frustrated by what he saw as a lack of effective war leadership by Aberdeen and the <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_War" title="Secretary of State for War">Secretary of State for War</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Henry_Pelham-Clinton,_5th_Duke_of_Newcastle" title="Henry Pelham-Clinton, 5th Duke of Newcastle">Duke of Newcastle</a>. Dispatches from the front reported that the army was suffering from supply shortages and a lack of adequate accommodation and medical facilities. In November 1854 Russell urged Aberdeen to replace Newcastle with Palmerston, who he believed would get a firmer grip on the organisation of the war, but these suggestions came to nothing. In January 1855, after a series of military setbacks, a Commons motion was brought by the radical MP <a href="/wiki/John_Arthur_Roebuck" title="John Arthur Roebuck">John Roebuck</a> to appoint a select committee to investigate the management of the war. Russell, not wishing to vote against an inquiry he believed was badly needed, resigned from the cabinet in order to abstain. Aberdeen viewed the Roebuck motion as a vote of no confidence in his leadership and, accordingly, when it passed by 305-148, he resigned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999225–229_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999225–229-51">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the eyes of many, including the Queen and Aberdeen, Russell's temperamental behaviour and personal ambition had undermined the stability of the coalition.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMartin1923107–112_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMartin1923107–112-52">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> On visiting <a href="/wiki/Windsor_Castle" title="Windsor Castle">Windsor Castle</a> to resign, Aberdeen told the Queen "Had it not been for the incessant attempts of Lord John Russell to keep up party differences, it must be acknowledged that the experiment of a coalition had succeeded admirably," an assessment with which the Queen agreed.<sup id="cite_ref-53" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-53">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup> Russell accepted an invitation from the Queen to form a new government but found that he could not assemble the necessary support, with many of his colleagues having been angered by his abandonment of Aberdeen over the Roebuck motion.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999229–230_54-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999229–230-54">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup> Palmerston became Prime Minister, and Russell reluctantly accepted the role of Colonial Secretary in his cabinet. Russell was sent to Vienna to negotiate peace terms with Russia, but his proposals were rejected and he resigned from the cabinet and returned to the backbenches in July 1855.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEVincent198137–49_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEVincent198137–49-55">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEArnold2002121_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEArnold2002121-56">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Return_to_the_backbenches:_1855.E2.80.931859"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_the_backbenches:_1855–1859">Return to the backbenches: 1855–1859</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Return to the backbenches: 1855–1859">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Following his resignation Russell wrote to his father-in-law that he would not serve again under Palmerston or any other Prime Minister.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999246_57-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999246-57">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> For a time it appeared as if his career in frontbench politics might be over. Russell continued to speak out from the backbenches on the issues he most cared about - lobbying for increased government grants for education and for reduction in the property qualification for Parliamentary elections.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999246–247_58-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999246–247-58">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup> In early 1857 Russell became a vocal critic of Palmerston's government over the <a href="/wiki/Anglo-Persian_War" title="Anglo-Persian War">Anglo-Persian War</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Second_Opium_War" title="Second Opium War">Second Opium War</a>. Russell spoke in support of a motion tabled by <a href="/wiki/Richard_Cobden" title="Richard Cobden">Richard Cobden</a>, which criticised British military action in China and calling for a select committee inquiry. When the motion passed on 3 March, Palmerston dissolved Parliament and went to the country.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid1895287_59-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid1895287-59">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup> In the subsequent <a href="/wiki/1857_United_Kingdom_general_election" title="1857 United Kingdom general election">general election</a> Palmerston was swept back into power on a tide of patriotic feeling with an increased majority. Many of Palmerston's critics lost their seats but Russell hung on in the City of London, after fighting off an attempt to deselect him and replace him with a pro-Palmerston Whig candidate.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999247–248_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999247–248-60">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup> Palmerston's triumph was short-lived. In February 1858 the Government rushed through a Conspiracy to Murder bill, following the <a href="/wiki/Orsini_plot" class="mw-redirect" title="Orsini plot">attempted assassination</a> of Napoleon III by Italian nationalist <a href="/wiki/Felice_Orsini" title="Felice Orsini">Felice Orsini</a> - an attack planned in Britain using British-made explosives. Russell attacked the bill, which he saw as undermined traditional British political liberties to appease a foreign government.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid1895290_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid1895290-61">&#91;60&#93;</a></sup> On 19 February Russell voted in favour of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Milner_Gibson" title="Thomas Milner Gibson">Thomas Milner Gibson's</a> motion, which criticised the government for bowing to French demands. When the motion passed by 19 votes Palmerston's government resigned.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPrest2009_3-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPrest2009-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Foreign_Secretary_under_Palmerston:_1859.E2.80.931865"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_Secretary_under_Palmerston:_1859–1865">Foreign Secretary under Palmerston: 1859–1865</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Foreign Secretary under Palmerston: 1859–1865">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Liberal_government,_1859%E2%80%931866" title="Liberal government, 1859–1866">Liberal government, 1859–1866</a></div>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/United_Kingdom_and_the_American_Civil_War" title="United Kingdom and the American Civil War">United Kingdom and the American Civil War</a></div>
<p>In 1859, following another short-lived Conservative government, Palmerston and Russell made up their differences, and Russell consented to serve as <a href="/wiki/Secretary_of_State_for_Foreign_Affairs_(UK)" class="mw-redirect" title="Secretary of State for Foreign Affairs (UK)">Foreign Secretary</a> in a new Palmerston cabinet, usually considered the first true <a href="/wiki/Liberal_Party_(UK)" title="Liberal Party (UK)">Liberal</a> cabinet. This period was a particularly eventful one in the world outside Britain, seeing the <a href="/wiki/Unification_of_Italy" title="Unification of Italy">Unification of Italy</a> (the change of British government to one sympathetic to Italian nationalism had a marked part in this process<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETrevelyan1909120-123_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETrevelyan1909120-123-62">&#91;61&#93;</a></sup>), the <a href="/wiki/American_Civil_War" title="American Civil War">American Civil War</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Second_Schleswig_War" title="Second Schleswig War">1864 war</a> over <a href="/wiki/Schleswig-Holstein" title="Schleswig-Holstein">Schleswig-Holstein</a> between <a href="/wiki/Denmark" title="Denmark">Denmark</a> and the <a href="/wiki/German_Confederation" title="German Confederation">German states</a>. Russell arranged the <a href="/wiki/London_Conference_of_1864" title="London Conference of 1864">London Conference of 1864</a>, but failed to establish peace in the war. His tenure of the Foreign Office was noteworthy for the famous dispatch in which he defended Italian unification: "Her Majesty's Government will turn their eyes rather to the gratifying prospect of a people building up the edifice of their liberties, and consolidating the work of their independence, amid the sympathies and good wishes of Europe" (27 October 1860).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid1895Ch._14_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid1895Ch._14-63">&#91;62&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Elevation_to_the_peerage:_1861">Elevation to the peerage: 1861</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=18" title="Edit section: Elevation to the peerage: 1861">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 1861 Russell was elevated to the peerage as <a href="/wiki/Earl_Russell" title="Earl Russell">Earl Russell</a>, of <a href="/wiki/Kingston_Russell" title="Kingston Russell">Kingston Russell</a> in the County of <a href="/wiki/Dorset" title="Dorset">Dorset</a>, and as <a href="/wiki/Viscount_Amberley" class="mw-redirect" title="Viscount Amberley">Viscount Amberley</a>, of <a href="/wiki/Amberley,_Gloucestershire" title="Amberley, Gloucestershire">Amberley</a> in the County of Gloucester, and of <a href="/w/index.php?title=Ardsalla&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Ardsalla (page does not exist)">Ardsalla</a> in the <a href="/wiki/County_Meath" title="County Meath">County of Meath</a>
in the <a href="/wiki/Peerage_of_the_United_Kingdom" title="Peerage of the United Kingdom">Peerage of the United Kingdom</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-64" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-64">&#91;63&#93;</a></sup> Henceforth, as a <a href="/wiki/Suo_jure" title="Suo jure">suo jure</a> peer, rather than merely being known as 'Lord' because he was the son of a Duke, he sat in the House of Lords for the remainder of his career.
</p>
<h2><span id="Prime_Minister_again:_1865.E2.80.931866"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Prime_Minister_again:_1865–1866">Prime Minister again: 1865–1866<span class="anchor" id="Second_premiership"></span></span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=19" title="Edit section: Prime Minister again: 1865–1866">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Further information: <a href="/wiki/Liberal_government,_1859%E2%80%931866" title="Liberal government, 1859–1866">Liberal government, 1859–1866</a></div>
<p>When Palmerston suddenly died in late 1865, Russell again became <a href="/wiki/Prime_minister" title="Prime minister">prime minister</a>. His second premiership was short and frustrating, and Russell failed in his great ambition of expanding the franchise, a task that would be left to his Conservative successors, Derby and <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Benjamin Disraeli</a>. In 1866, party disunity again brought down his government. Russell never again held any office. His last contribution to the House of Lords was on 3 August 1875.<sup id="cite_ref-65" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-65">&#91;64&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Personal_life">Personal life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Personal life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Marriages_and_children">Marriages and children</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=21" title="Edit section: Marriages and children">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Lady_John_Russell.png" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Lady_John_Russell.png/170px-Lady_John_Russell.png" decoding="async" width="170" height="212" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Lady_John_Russell.png/255px-Lady_John_Russell.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cc/Lady_John_Russell.png/340px-Lady_John_Russell.png 2x" data-file-width="351" data-file-height="438" /></a><figcaption>Russell's first wife Adelaide (1807-1838)</figcaption></figure>
<p>Russell married Adelaide Lister (widow of <a href="/wiki/Thomas_Lister,_2nd_Baron_Ribblesdale" title="Thomas Lister, 2nd Baron Ribblesdale">Thomas Lister, 2nd Baron Ribblesdale</a>, who had died in 1832<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer199980–82_66-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer199980–82-66">&#91;65&#93;</a></sup>) on 11 April 1835. Together they had two daughters:
</p>
<ul><li>Lady Georgiana Adelaide Russell (1836 – 25 September 1922). She married Archibald Peel (son of General <a href="/wiki/Jonathan_Peel" title="Jonathan Peel">Jonathan Peel</a>) on 15 August 1867. They had seven children.</li>
<li>Lady Victoria Russell (20 October 1838 – 9 May 1880). She married Henry Villiers (the son of The Honorable <a href="/wiki/Henry_Montagu_Villiers" title="Henry Montagu Villiers">Henry Montagu Villiers</a>) on 16 April 1861. They had ten children and left many descendants.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEReid1895_67-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEReid1895-67">&#91;66&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<p>Adelaide came down with a fever following the birth of their second child and died a few days later on 1 November 1838. Following her death, Russell continued to raise his late wife's four children from her first marriage, as well their two daughters.
</p>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:Frances,_Lady_John_Russell.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Frances%2C_Lady_John_Russell.jpg/170px-Frances%2C_Lady_John_Russell.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="258" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Frances%2C_Lady_John_Russell.jpg/255px-Frances%2C_Lady_John_Russell.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/69/Frances%2C_Lady_John_Russell.jpg/340px-Frances%2C_Lady_John_Russell.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2200" data-file-height="3344" /></a><figcaption>Russell's second wife Frances with their eldest son John</figcaption></figure>
<p>On 20 July 1841 Russell remarried, to <a href="/wiki/Frances_Russell,_Countess_Russell" title="Frances Russell, Countess Russell">Lady Frances ("Fanny") Elliot-Murray-Kynynmound</a>, daughter of Russell's cabinet colleague <a href="/wiki/Gilbert_Elliot,_2nd_Earl_of_Minto" class="mw-redirect" title="Gilbert Elliot, 2nd Earl of Minto">Gilbert Elliot, 2nd Earl of Minto</a>. Together they had four children:
</p>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/John_Russell,_Viscount_Amberley" title="John Russell, Viscount Amberley">John Russell, Viscount Amberley</a> (10 December 1842 – 9 January 1876). He married <a href="/wiki/Katharine_Russell,_Viscountess_Amberley" title="Katharine Russell, Viscountess Amberley">The Hon. Katherine Stanley</a> on 8 November 1864. They had four children, including a stillborn daughter. Their eldest son, <a href="/wiki/Frank_Russell,_2nd_Earl_Russell" title="Frank Russell, 2nd Earl Russell">Frank</a>, would succeed Lord John to the title to become the 2nd Earl Russell. Another son, the 3rd Earl, was the philosopher <a href="/wiki/Bertrand_Russell" title="Bertrand Russell">Bertrand Russell</a>.</li>
<li>Hon. George Gilbert William Russell (14 April 1848 – 27 January 1933).</li>
<li>Hon. <a href="/wiki/Rollo_Russell" title="Rollo Russell">Francis Albert Rollo Russell</a> (11 July 1849 – 30 March 1914). He married Alice Godfrey (d. 12 May 1886) on 21 April 1885. They had one son. He remarried Gertrude Joachim on 28 April 1891. They had two children.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Lady_Agatha_Russell" title="Lady Agatha Russell">Lady Mary Agatha Russell</a> (1853 – 23 April 1933).</li></ul>
<p>In 1847 <a href="/wiki/Queen_Victoria" title="Queen Victoria">Queen Victoria</a> granted <a href="/wiki/Pembroke_Lodge" class="mw-redirect" title="Pembroke Lodge">Pembroke Lodge</a> in <a href="/wiki/Richmond_Park" title="Richmond Park">Richmond Park</a> to Lord and Lady John. It remained their family home for the rest of their lives.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEScherer1999135_68-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEScherer1999135-68">&#91;67&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Fletcher_Jones_69-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Fletcher_Jones-69">&#91;68&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Religious_views">Religious views</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=22" title="Edit section: Religious views">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Russell was religious in a simple non-dogmatic way and supported "<a href="/wiki/Broad_church" title="Broad church">broad church</a>" stances in the Church of England. He opposed the "<a href="/wiki/Oxford_Movement" title="Oxford Movement">Oxford Movement</a>" because its "Tractarian" members were too dogmatic and too close to <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Roman Catholicism</a>. He supported Broad Churchmen or Latitudinarians by several appointments of liberal churchmen as bishops. In 1859 he reversed himself and decided to free non-Anglicans of the duty of paying rates (taxes) to the local Anglican parish. His political clumsiness and opposition to Church finance made him a target of attack and ridicule in many Church circles.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENikol1974341–357_70-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENikol1974341–357-70">&#91;69&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEllens1987232–249_71-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEllens1987232–249-71">&#91;70&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEChadwick1966129,_146,_479_72-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEChadwick1966129,_146,_479-72">&#91;71&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Final_years_and_death">Final years and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=23" title="Edit section: Final years and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Following the death of their daughter-in-law Viscountess Amberley in 1874 and their son Viscount Amberley in 1876, Earl Russell and Countess Russell brought up their orphaned grandchildren, <a href="/wiki/Frank_Russell,_2nd_Earl_Russell" title="Frank Russell, 2nd Earl Russell">John ("Frank") Russell</a>, who became 2nd Earl Russell on his grandfather's death, and <a href="/wiki/Bertrand_Russell" title="Bertrand Russell">Bertrand Russell</a> who would go on to become a noted philosopher and who in later life recalled his elderly grandfather as "a kindly old man in a wheelchair."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClark2011Ch._1_73-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark2011Ch._1-73">&#91;72&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Earl Russell died at home at Pembroke Lodge on 28 May 1878. The Prime Minister, the <a href="/wiki/Benjamin_Disraeli" title="Benjamin Disraeli">Earl of Beaconsfield</a>, offered a public funeral and burial at Westminster Abbey for Russell but this was declined by Countess Russell in accordance with her late husband's wish to be buried among his family and ancestors.<sup id="cite_ref-74" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-74">&#91;73&#93;</a></sup> He is buried at the <a href="/wiki/St_Michael%27s,_Chenies#Bedford_Chapel" title="St Michael&#39;s, Chenies">'Bedford Chapel'</a> at <a href="/wiki/St_Michael%27s,_Chenies" title="St Michael&#39;s, Chenies">St. Michael's Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Chenies" title="Chenies">Chenies</a>, Buckinghamshire.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy_and_reputation">Legacy and reputation</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=John_Russell,_1st_Earl_Russell&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Legacy and reputation">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size mw-halign-left" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:John_Russell,_Vanity_Fair,_1869-06-05.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/John_Russell%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-06-05.jpg/210px-John_Russell%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-06-05.jpg" decoding="async" width="210" height="334" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/John_Russell%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-06-05.jpg/315px-John_Russell%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-06-05.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e2/John_Russell%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-06-05.jpg/420px-John_Russell%2C_Vanity_Fair%2C_1869-06-05.jpg 2x" data-file-width="448" data-file-height="712" /></a><figcaption>"The greatest liberal statesman of modern times" - Russell's small stature was frequently lampooned by political caricaturists.</figcaption></figure>
<p>Scion of one of the most powerful aristocratic families, Russell was a leading reformer who weakened the power of the aristocracy. His great achievements, wrote <a href="/wiki/A._J._P._Taylor" title="A. J. P. Taylor">A. J. P. Taylor</a>, were based on his persistent battles in Parliament over the years on behalf of the expansion of liberty; after each loss he tried again and again, until finally, his efforts were largely successful.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETaylor197667_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETaylor197667-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Llewellyn_Woodward" title="Llewellyn Woodward">E. L. Woodward</a>, however, argued that he was too much the abstract theorist:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>He was more concerned with the removal of obstacles to civil liberty than with the creation of a more reasonable and civilized society.
